ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u1	Dear fellow Members and fellow Members, ladies and gentlemen! Starting the 20th meeting of the National Assembly. I am informed that today's meetings cannot be attended by the following Members and Members: Violeta Tomić, Ksenija Korenjak Kramar, Dr. Milan Brglez between 13 and 15 p.m., Dr. Bojan Dobovšek, Matjaž Hanžek, Dr. Franc Trček, Jernej Vrtovac until 6 p.m. and Dr. Vinko Gorenak from 13 p.m. I say hello to everyone who's here! We're moving on to 13. On the other hand, this is a proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Act on Payment Services and Systems following the ordinary procedure. The draft law was tabled by the Government for consideration by the National Assembly. In order to further explain the proposal, I give the floor to the representative of the Government, State Secretary at the Ministry of Finance, Mrs Irena Sodin.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u2	Dear President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! The main objective of the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Law on Payment Services and Systems is to transpose into Slovenian law the Directive on the comparability of payments related to payment accounts, exchange of payment accounts and access to basic payment accounts. The law also transposes the provisions of the Regulation on the introduction of technical and business requirements for credit transfers and direct debits in euro and the provisions of the Regulation implementing the Regulation on interbank commissions for card payment transactions. In addition, the proposal for a law with minor amendments and additions to the Law on Payment Services and Systems improves the current legal framework. In connection with the transposition of the Directive, the proposal regulates three areas: it lays down rules on the comparability of payments related to payment accounts; it lays down rules on the replacement of payment accounts; and it lays down rules on access to basic payment accounts. In terms of the comparability of payment account compensation, the proposal provides in more detail the form, manner and time limits in which payment service providers must provide the consumer with free information on payment account compensations. On its website, Banka Slovenije will allow consumers to compare the fees charged by payment service providers in the Republic of Slovenia for free. Comparing refunds on its website Banka Slovenije publishes already, but in future this publication will comply with the requirements of the Directive. Reimbursements will also be published for services on the final list with standardised terminology to be determined by the European Commission by issuing regulatory technical standards. Payment service providers will have to use these standardised terms for each payment service, thus contributing to greater transparency of payment services and facilitating consumers to compare payments between individual providers. The bill also regulates the replacement of the payment account within a Member State and the cross-border replacement. At the request of the consumer, the replacement procedure within the Republic of Slovenia will be initiated by the receiving payment service provider, i.e. the provider in which the consumer will open a new payment account. The receiving and transferring payment service provider will be obliged to carry out all procedures to replace the payment account specified in the consumer’s mandate and within the statutory time limits. The bill introduces a new category of payment accounts. These are basic payment accounts. The Basic Payment Account is an account with a specified set of services as defined in the Directive. The opening of the basic payment account will have to be offered by all banks in the Republic of Slovenia under non-discriminatory conditions. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia proposes to the National Assembly that the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the law be adopted. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u5	The following is a presentation of the views of the groups of Members. The text is given by the Parliamentary Group of the Democratic Party of the Pensioners of Slovenia, for which Mr Uroš Prikl has spoken.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u6	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Dear colleagues and colleagues! Allow me to brief you on the position of the Desus Group on the proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Law on Payment Services and Systems, which we will unanimously support in our Group. The main objective of the bill proposal is to transpose the Directive on the comparability of payments related to payment accounts, exchange of payment accounts and access to basic payment accounts. In addition, it will legislate, namely, the provisions of the Regulation on the introduction of technical and business requirements for credit transfers and direct debits in euro and the provisions of the Interbank Fees Regulation and card payment transactions. The bill is for the benefit of the consumer and this is of crucial importance to us. In the Desus Group we welcome the solution which will oblige Banka Slovenije to publish a list on its website and to allow for a comparison of refunds free of charge. Such a solution will, in our firm belief, help consumers to decide for the most cost-effective payment service provider, and this will also affect greater competition between individual payment service providers. The latter will have to provide the consumer with at least once a year free of charge for all fees charged for payment account services, with standardised terms to be used. The bill also regulates the replacement of the payment account within a Member State and the cross-border replacement. The exchange of a payment account will have to be made possible by a payment service provider to any consumer who opens or has a payment account with a payment service provider in our country. Any compensation to be charged to the consumer by the transferee or the receiving payment service provider for any service under the substitution procedure will have to be appropriate and in accordance with the actual costs of the payment service provider, and this will prevent the possible benevolence of consumers or of the beneficiaries. Banks will have to reject the request to open a basic account if the opening of such an account would result in a breach of the provisions of the law governing the prevention and laundering of money and the financing of terrorism, allowing for the imprudentness or even deliberate possible tampering of the system. As has already been said, in the Desus Group, the proposal for a law seems to us to be a step in the right direction, as it will bring greater transparency to consumers regarding the charging of refunds, facilitate the comparison of their level between individual providers and facilitate the replacement of the payment account. Therefore, transparency, efficiency and elimination of administrative barriers – with one word: order. For this reason, we will unanimously support the proposed Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on Payment Services and Systems in the Desus Group. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u7	The text is given by the Group of Social Democrats, Mr Matjaž Germany.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u8	Thank you, President. Colleagues and colleagues! The European Directive on the comparability of payments for exchange and access to payment accounts refers to this problem from the perspective of the consumer. This is an area that can be classified as an emergency service without access to which it is not possible to operate normally in modern society. Like telephony, payment traffic has become a necessity, even those without electronic banking must pay the bills, which are also part of this, often a very opaque system. We all use them, and we all pay for them. However, it is very difficult to assess how different all these services are by name, content and quality. The Directive thus introduces a uniform expression and obligations for all payment service providers to present them to the consumer in a standardised manner and for regulators to ensure that prices can be compared by different typical services. The new Law on Payment Services and Systems, which transposes the European Directive into the Slovenian legal order, is therefore particularly important for the protection of consumers who use payment services and systems on a daily basis. In view of the wide-ranging electronicisation of business and life, they could even talk about the dependency of residents and residents on payment services and systems and, as a result, of their managers of banks, brokerage houses, traders and other payment service providers. Income from work, an essential part of human property and savings depends on the proper functioning of these systems. This is an emergency service where more or less hidden costs often occur. The solutions provided by such a recast law include, inter alia, the unification of technology, services and a single universally accessible benchmark to be published by Banka Slovenije, a standardised price comparison with which banks will have to inform consumers on each contract signing and the regular updating of the contract. Banks will have to issue annual transparent accounts of all fees charged to their clients. One of the most important services, however, follows as much as possible the need for a universally accessible basic payment account that could be obtained by any resident. Payment service providers will thus have to offer a basic payment account under non-discriminatory conditions and the restrictions are only related to the broadcasting and prevention of misuse of terrorist financing and money laundering. Only in these cases will the banks be allowed to refuse the opening of the basic payment account. Much time was devoted to the debate on transparency and mutual comparison in the treatment of the parent body. There was a proposal that price comparisons should also be published by social work centres. This is certainly a good proposal for access of the poorest victims and the availability of quality information, and we also welcome the agreement that information on price comparisons will also be published on the sides of the Ministry of Labour, Family, Social Affairs and Equal Opportunities. Thank you very much.

The proposed law will introduce a basic bank account, which will focus in particular on socially disadvantaged people. The basic idea is good, it is an account with a limited number of transactions that should be sufficient for basic needs. So far, we agree with the proposal. The problem is that the measure has not been implemented consistently. European regulations provide that the basic account is free of charge or that the bank can only charge the prescribed costs. Since this is a social measure, the logical solution would be that the basic account is free of charge. However, as soon as the charging of services is allowed, the size of the Bank of Slovenia's part is increased, which must prescribe and monitor prices. At the same time with such a system we cause people to deal with price comparisons by banks, although differences are expressed in cents, and I am sure that both people and Banka Slovenije have much more important work. Our proposal would therefore be much simpler: basic accounts should be free of charge and the prices of other accounts should be prescribed. At the meeting of the parent committee, we tried to mislead the implementation of the law in this direction, but our amendments were rejected, both as an amendment to prevent banks from charging the costs of implementation to socially disadvantaged people, as an amendment that the Bank of Slovenia must take social criteria into account when setting the maximum permitted prices. It is a matter of preventing socially disadvantaged residents of the bank from squandering funds, but they still charge them the cost of looking; otherwise they must not recover this in the current month, but they always do so in the next month. Some banks also charged EUR 30 or more for such processing of the enforcement decision. The coalition of these two amendments was not prepared to support it, so we also cancelled support for this law. Therefore, according to our assessment, it is a measure which has been half implemented that will not achieve its purpose. This is also confirmed by the experience with the closure of register accounts for securities. Last year, the coalition adopted a new Law on Securities, which regulates the registration accounts. We have always opposed the abolition because it is solely in the interest of the stockbrokers. In the third reading, an amendment was adopted limiting the amount of compensation that the broker may charge. By that time, we had warned that stockbrokers would pass this limit. That's what happened. Our citizens are receiving offers from stock brokers to open a commercial account these days. One of the largest brokerage houses has recently sent an action offer to charge €10 for the transfer by the end of August, after which the cost of opening and maintaining the account will be over €40. I'm not saying this to do advertising, but to show on this occasion that the legal restriction imposed there does not work. The law limited the costs of transferring from registration to a commercial account, but stockbrokers charge the opening and maintenance of a commercial account, statement of status and the like. According to the latest data, natural persons still have 155 000 open accounts, and if we multiply it by costs, it says 40 euros for opening and managing, we see. What a gift the coalition has granted to the stockbrokers. Since we are confident that banks with private accounts will also earn more than the real costs, and because the amendments, in particular the amendment which has been pointed out many times in civil society, have not been supported, we cannot support this law.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u11	The text is given by the New Slovenia Group - Christian Democrats, for which Mr Jožef Horvat has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u12	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Dear representatives of the Ministry of Finance, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! The main purpose of the proposed amendment of the Law on Payment Services and Systems is to transpose the relevant Directive of the European Union and three other European regulations into the Slovenian legal order. The bill provides for harmonisation of terminology, provides for the obligation of the Bank of Slovenia to publish on its website a list of standardised terminology for the most representative services related to the payment account for which compensation is paid. The list of standardised terminology will also have to be provided free of charge to the consumer by each payment service provider. The payment service provider will have to provide the consumer at least once a year with a free invoice of all fees charged for the payment account-related services. All of these solutions are seen by Christian Democrats as a positive measure to increase the transparency of business. On its website, the Bank of Slovenia will allow consumers to compare refunds. This will make it easier for users to choose a bank, which certainly promotes competition between service providers. As regards the provision of the basic payment account, the Bank will only be able to charge the consumer an appropriate compensation. The draft law provides that the Bank of Slovenia will, by means of a sub-law, prescribe a way of calculating the amount of the appropriate compensation. The exchange of a payment account will have to enable any payment service provider to open or hold a payment account with a payment service provider in the Republic of Slovenia. Any compensation to be charged to the consumer by the port or receiving payment service provider for any service in the substitution procedure will have to be appropriate and in accordance with the actual costs of the payment service provider. The whole substitution procedure shall not take longer than 12 working days. The proposed amendment facilitates the transition of consumers between providers, which we understand as a measure to actually improve the functioning of competition in the banking sector. In the case of cross-border replacements, the payment service provider, where the consumer has a payment account, will also have to provide certain relevant information free of charge. In Novi Slovenija we will support the proposal for a law on changes and additions to the Law on Payment Services and Systems.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u13	The word is given by the Group of the Modern Center Party, for her mag. Dušan Verbich.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u14	Thank you for your word, President. Dear Secretary of State, Secretary of State, Members and Members of Parliament, a very nice greeting! Today we are considering amendments and additions to the Law on Payment Services and Systems. With regard to the current arrangements, it is necessary to clarify that the applicable law regulates the condition for the establishment, operation, control and termination of payment institutions, the rights and obligations of users and providers of these services in connection with the provision of payment services; the rules on the design and management of the systems themselves; the conditions for the management of electronic money issuance activities; and the conditions for the establishment, operation, control and termination of electronic money companies. This bill, however, brings to the Slovenian legal order a directive and regulations governing the introduction of technical and business requirements for credit transfers and direct debits in euro and interbank commissions for card payment transactions. In connection with the transposition of the Directive, the proposal for a law governs three areas. Firstly, it lays down rules on the comparability of payments related to payment accounts, secondly, rules on the replacement of payment accounts and thirdly, rules on access to basic payment accounts. The bill provides that Banka Slovenije will publish on its website a list of standardised terminology for the most representative services related to the payment account for which compensation is paid. This list will have to be aligned with the terminology of the final list prepared by the European Banking Authority and will then be published by the European Commission in the form of regulatory technical standards. In addition to the list, a common symbol will be developed to distinguish the compensation document from other documents. A list of standardised terminology will have to be provided free of charge to each payment service provider before the conclusion of the framework contract for the management of the payment account and ensure that the compensation information document is always available to the consumer. The payment service provider will also have to use standardised terms with the final list in its contracts with consumers and in advertising. The proposal will facilitate the replacement of the payment account for consumers as it prescribes the procedures to be carried out by the transfer and receiving payment service provider when changing the account and sets the time limits within which individual procedures are to be carried out. The bill also grants consumers the right to open a basic payment account that will enable consumers to carry out all basic payment services at a reasonable price. We support the proposal of the Modern Centre Party in the Parliamentary Group mainly because the law will bring greater transparency in calculating the remuneration of the payment service provider in managing them and facilitate the comparison of their level between individual providers, helping the consumer to decide for the most cost-effective payment service provider. This will also affect greater competition between individual payment service providers in the Republic of Slovenia. As it is said, the SMC Group supports the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Law on Payment Services and Systems. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u16	Thank you, President, for your word. Hello, everyone in the hall! The basic objective of the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Law on Payment Services and Systems is to transpose the European Directive on the comparability of payments related to payment accounts, exchange of payment accounts and access to basic payment accounts. The bill provides that Banka Slovenije will have to publish on its website a standardised list for the most representative services related to the payment account for which compensation is paid. This list will have to be aligned with the terminology of the final list prepared by the European Banking Authority and will then be published by the European Commission in the form of technical standards. Each payment service provider will have to provide the consumer free of charge before concluding a framework contract to manage the payment account and ensure that the compensation information document is always available to the consumer. The payment service provider will also have to use standardised terms from the final list in its contracts with consumers and also in advertising. At least once a year, the service provider will also have to provide the consumer with a free payment account of all the fees charged for the services related to the payment account. The proposal also regulates the replacement of the payment account within a Member State and the cross-border replacement, which will begin at the request of the consumer. Consumers are expected to be given a better review of the costs of their business with the bank and to facilitate the comparability of the banking offer, while also reducing the barriers to consumer mobility represented by the replacement of the bank. However, the law also has certain shortcomings. It is impossible to access basic payment accounts to consumers who are in the execution process, the methodology for certain basic payment account operating prices is not designed in a way that can also be afforded by socially disadvantaged groups, and information on the right to a basic payment account is not available in several places – here I am referring to the exposure of social work centres, employment institutions and so on. We will not oppose the proposal of the law in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u19	The proposal for a recommendation was treated by the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy as a parent working body. For the presentation of the report of the Committee, I give the floor to the President of Mrs Urska Ban.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u20	Thank you so much for saying that. Dear colleagues and colleagues, representatives of the Ministry! The Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy shall be held at its 24th meeting, on 25 June. 5. 2016, as a parent working body, considered the proposal for that recommendation submitted to the National Assembly by a group of Members and Members with first-signed Jozet Tank. In addition to the proposal for a recommendation, the Committee also received an opinion from the Government of the Republic of Slovenia of 6. 5, material with the number 450-03/16-25/6 bearing the trade secret code in the safety deposit box in the Udis system of 9. 5. 2016 and the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service of 11. 5. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. No amendments were tabled on the proposal for a recommendation. Representatives of the Ministry of Finance, Banka Slovenije, DUTB and Legal and Legal Services attended the meeting. The initial supplementary explanation of the proposal was presented by SDS Member Marko Pogačnik, on behalf of the applicant, who presented the content of the proposal for a recommendation and the reasons for the proposed recommendation. He drew attention to the report of the DUTB Management Board of February 2016 transmitted to the Government of the Republic of Slovenia stating that DUTB would create an additional loss of between €40 million and €50 million due to the accession of the Factor Bank and Probank. In addition, he pointed out that the decision of the Government as well as Banka Slovenije regarding the simplified connection between Factor Bank and Probanka to DUTB was surprising and highlighted the question of possible recapitalisation of DUTB due to this connection. The representative of the Legal and Legislative Service took note of the comments made in the written opinion and drew attention to the fact that the Government should also ensure their confidentiality in accordance with the rules when submitting any confidential documents. The State Secretary of the Ministry of Finance presented the Government's written opinion, pointing out that the above findings in the recommendation were unfounded and that the Government was informed of the financial situation of the two banks. He also justified the Government's decision on the way in which the merger was carried out, which was legally and economically justified and also transparent. He said that the Government was not opposed to the recommendation that the Government should forward the opening balance sheet of the two banks to the National Assembly, as well as to forward the DUB report to the National Assembly. With regard to paragraphs 3 and 4 of the Recommendation, however, the Government considers that there are no reasons for the proposed discharge. The Governor of Banka Slovenije said that all decisions and procedures under the responsibility of Banka Slovenije have been carried out prudently, with the utmost care to maintain the stability of the financial system also in terms of the consumption of tax money. He further detailed the reasons why it was decided that Factor Bank and Probank had gone into controlled liquidation and not bankruptcy. He said that the initial assessment of the costs of liquidation of both banks was estimated at EUR 400 million, with all data not yet known at the time. The final amount of the minimum necessary recapitalisation is EUR 445 million and was also confirmed by the European Commission in November 2013. However, the recapitalisation was carried out in December 2013. He estimated that the final cost of the closure of the two banks for the budget of the Republic of Slovenia would be equal to the amount of the recapitalisation carried out in December 2013, namely EUR 445 million. This amount shall be at the lower limit of the then envisaged recapitalisation scenario. The remaining EUR 76 million of approved recapitalisation remains, while at the same time the cost of recapitalisation, i.e. EUR 445 million, is around EUR 300 million lower than the cost if bankruptcy were imposed on banks. The discussion raised questions about the need for additional recapitalisation of the DUTB due to the addition of both banks, the methods used to assess bad claims, the possible other solutions for both banks, the large amounts held by state institutions in these two banks, and the issue of subordinated bonds. A question was also raised regarding the Bank of Slovenia's positive assessment of the operation of both banks one year before the liquidations were controlled and the responsibility for the preparation of the materials on the basis of which the Council of the Bank of Slovenia decides. In the discussion below, the members and members of the Committee also highlighted the issue of the transfer of employees from both banks to the DUTB and the costs involved. Representatives of the Ministry of Finance and Banka Slovenije were responsible for the discussion. In accordance with the Rules of Procedure, the Committee adopted points 2 and 1, 3 and 4 of the Recommendation and was not adopted by the Committee. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u21	For an introductory explanation of the opinion, I give the floor to the representative of the Government, State Secretary at the Ministry of Finance, Mr Method Dragonji.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u22	Mr President, thank you for the floor. Dear Members and Members! The proposal for recommendations under consideration relates to the accession of Probanka and Factor Bank to the Company for the Management of Bank Claims. This is a topic that has already been explained by the Government and the Ministry of Finance, not only on the home working body of the Finance Committee, but also on the Commission for the supervision of public finances, but also a number of detailed answers to individual parliamentary questions. At its meeting on 17 February 2016 as the Probanke and Factor Bank Assembly, the Government adopted conclusions on the simplification of the association of the two companies with the Bank's Claims Management Company. The Government underlines that this decision has been taken carefully and weighed on the basis of all necessary information and preliminary decisions. This approach has proved to be the most appropriate in terms of the total capital burden for the country and the public financial impact. This also took into account the fact that, as of September 2013, both banks, on a proposal from the Bank of Slovenia, were in a controlled winding-up procedure which must be completed in accordance with a decision of the European Commission granting State aid to banks by 31 at the latest. 12. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. The current government could not interfere with the decision taken in 2013 by the Bank of Slovenia and the then Government on the emergency measures taken to introduce a process for the controlled cessation of both banks. At the time of its term of office, the government has been dealing with the most rational way to close the operations of both banks and with the lowest additional burden for the state budget and taxpayers. The findings of the proposers of the recommendation are assessed as unfounded, and we stress that the whole accession process has been carried out in a transparent manner. Connections were made on the basis of revised balance sheets and with due diligence. At the same time, Banka Slovenije, in its assessment of the connection prepared as the supervisory board of Probanka and Factor Banka, assessed that the connection was legally and economically justified and found that the accession procedures were carried out in accordance with the provisions of the Companies Act. The Management Board of the Banks Claims Management Company, in its report on the accession of the Government as a meeting, pointed out that further weaknesses in the transferred credit lines of the two banks will be required due to a stricter methodology to determine the fair value of the assets and due to the higher capital cost of DUTB. The Ministry of Finance estimates that the additional weaknesses in the amounts transferred on the accession items will not exceed the amount approved but not the State aid used by both banks by the European Commission. In addition to minimising the capital burden for the country, the Ministry of Finance has pursued another, very important objective, namely to ensure the full payment of liquidity aid to both banks, which was granted in the form of an emergency loan by the European Central Bank in 2013. For both loans amounting to EUR 30 million, she issued a guarantee from the Republic of Slovenia. In 2014, the guarantee was cashed at an unpaid loan of EUR 428 million and two credit contracts were concluded with the Ministry of Finance for further payment of this loan. The attachment of both banks to DUTB allows the remaining assets of both banks to be cashed in step-by-step and not on liquidation or bankruptcy terms, thereby eliminating the risk that the aid in the form of a liquidity loan would not be fully reimbursed. The government does not oppose the proposal to recommend to the Government that we submit to the National Assembly the report of the Banks Claims Management Company. With the addition of Factor Bank and Probank, the complexity of the operation of this institution has increased, and it will therefore be necessary to ensure that DUTB receives adequate support to fund the remaining assets of both banks and that both objectives of these procedures are successfully achieved. First, the reduction of the capital burden for the country and secondly, the full payment of the liquidity aid paid to the banks. In the process of controlled liquidation of the two banks, the depositories of the two banks were paid in full, enabling them to cease and, on the other hand, the negative effect of the termination of the two banks on the financial system of the Republic of Slovenia was prevented and financial stability ensured, which was one of the basic conditions for recovery of the economy. Thank you very much.

Several possible ways and multiple possible termination scenarios have been assessed. First, to withdraw only a bank licence and to keep these banks operating; not as banks, but as normal companies, or to impose bankruptcy proceedings on them. The second option to ensure full recapitalisation, as approved by the European Commission, is that the total amount of State aid granted has not yet been used. And the third option is to attach these two banks to the Bank Management Company or so-called bad banks. The implementation of the merger procedure of Factor Bank and Probank is expected to represent the most favourable option in terms of the overall burden on the State. ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u31	The word is given by the Group of the Modern Center Party, for her mag. Bojan Krajnc.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u32	Dear Mr President, distinguished Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! At its regular meeting on 17 February 2016 as the Assembly of Factor Bank and Probanke, the Government adopted conclusions on the simplification of the link between the two banks and the Company for the Management of Bank Claims. It has taken its decision on the basis of all necessary information and preliminary decisions in this regard, as it has proved to be the most appropriate approach in terms of the overall capital burden for the country and the public financial impact. Both banks have been in a controlled termination process since 6 September 2013 at the request of the Bank of Slovenia. In accordance with the liquidation plan, the banks rapidly divested most of their assets and reduced their liabilities, with their assets ultimately consisting of non-performing loans, less liquid shares and shares, and a real estate portfolio, which is linked to long-term enforcement and insolvency proceedings. In the light of the decision of the European Commission authorising State aid, controlled terminations should be completed by 31 December 2016 at the latest, and the banks should therefore begin to sell or cash their remaining assets more rapidly, which could mean that prices achieved would be significantly lower than they could have been achieved over a longer period of time. Therefore, the early completion of the controlled winding-up procedure by means of their accession to the DUTB is in the function of reaching the maximum price for the remaining assets of the banks and thus paying all their creditors to the maximum extent possible. The recapitalisation of EUR 269 million and Probanks amounting to EUR 176 million has been carried out and it should be stressed that the European Commission has approved higher recapitalisation amounts. For Factor Bank, EUR 285 million and for Probank, EUR 236 million, which means that state aid has not been fully used – for Probank at EUR 60 million and for Factor Bank at EUR 16 million. In addition, a State guarantee was granted for the liabilities of the Bank and Probanks to the Bank of Slovenia for the drawing of a direct loan in extreme force — a guarantee of up to EUR 540 million for Factor Bank and a guarantee of up to EUR 490 million for Probank. On 19 June 2014, on the basis of the guarantee agreement, the Bank of Slovenia called on the Ministry of Finance to pay a guarantee to the Bank at almost EUR 187 million and to Probank at a value of EUR 241 million because the banks did not repay the loan. After the guarantee was cashed, a claim from the Republic of Slovenia to both banks was made and the addition of both banks to the DUTB eliminates the risk that such liquidity aid to the banks of the Republic of Slovenia would not be reimbursed and, in the event of bankruptcy, such risks would certainly exist. After the implementation of the accession to the Republic of Slovenia, the debt under the form of a cash guarantee shall be reported. DUTB has also recently repaid EUR 150 million of liabilities to the Ministry of Finance, which it took over with the addition of Probank and Factor Bank. The prudence of the method and transparency of the implementation of the merger procedure stems from the following two essential facts. The linkage was based on the latest audited financial statements, as both banks prepared a revised final report, in accordance with Article 68 of the Company Act, on the day of the merger, on 1 January 2016, and a revised annual report for 2015. All these documents, together with the reports of the supervisory boards of the two banks on the intended accession to the DUB, have already been received in a protected drawer in the Udis system on 9 May 2016. It should also be pointed out that the Company Act does not require the preparation of other revised balance sheets in the case of an attachment not carried out in a simplified manner. There is also no need to draw up an interim balance. The interim balance sheet was not mandatory because the condition of Article 586(2) of the Company Act, which provides that the interim balance sheet should be drawn up only if the last annual report of the individual companies involved in the accession relates to a business year which ended more than six months before the conclusion of the accession contract. This condition regarding the connection of Factor Bank and Probank was not met, which means that the interim balance sheet is not necessary. The following essential fact, resulting in the prudence of the method and transparency of the implementation of the connection procedure, stems from the Bank of Slovenia's assessment of the connection prepared as the Supervisory Board of Probanke and Factor Bank. It wrote: "On the basis of a review of all relevant documents, including the revised 2015 annual report and the revised annual report of the company taken over on day 1. 1. 2016, after obtaining all the necessary information and data affecting the connections and analysing the reasons and consequences of the accession of the Council of the Bank of Slovenia in the exercise of the supervisory board of the Bank and Probank, estimates that the link in question is economically viable for the Republic of Slovenia as the sole owner of the two companies, as the connection ensures a rationalisation of the business and, in particular, the extension of the asset repurchase period of the company taken over and thus a higher cut and lower probability of additional capital burdens for the sole owner. The Council of the Bank of Slovenia therefore considers that the proposed accession is legally and economically justified and notes that the accession procedures so far are carried out in accordance with the provisions of the Company Act." End of quote. In its report on the accession in February of 3 June 2016, which we also received in a protected drawer in the Udis system, the DUTB Board noted that it could call on the owner to replace the transferred capital deficit of both banks of the Republic of Slovenia. This is related to the methodology for the valuation of the transferred portfolio from both banks to DUTB, which differs slightly from the methodology used by both banks. However, the balance sheet of both banks was audited by the authorised auditors and the DUTB used a different capital cost in its valuation models than both banks used in the controlled winding-up process. The position regarding the possible recapitalisation of the DUTB in the context of the connection deficit will thus be taken when the DUTB completes the evaluation of the transferred items of Probanka and Factor Bank and will give an opinion to the auditor auditing the DUTB financial statements. It follows from all this that decisions on the purpose of liquidity aid and the recapitalisation of banks were taken in previous mandates and that the current government sought to reduce the burden on public finances as much as possible by connecting. She did so in a wise and transparent way. We will support the second recommendation in the Group of the Parties of the Modern Centre. As has already been said, there is really no need for this because the documents have already been transmitted. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u34	Honourable President of the National Assembly, Honourable Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! Which, of course, means that if we now swear that everything was transparent and so made, we must also say, of course, that something has not been done in accordance with what this National Assembly or its Commission has adopted. The government as the executive did not actually do so and therefore there is no interim report and there are no intermediate balances either on the Bank’s side or on the Probank’s side and, on the other hand, there are no intermediate balances with the Bank’s Claims Management Company. All of this then raises doubts about this and, of course, with the view that everything is okay, that everything is transparent, that we have done everything, we are somehow putting ourselves in a position like we have no role to play. This is probably the most or very worrying situation today, in addition to the fact that this simplified merger pays an additional EUR 50 million in taxpayer money to the Company for the Management of Bank Claims. From this address! Here, of course, we must not neglect this fact that the Company for Bank Claims Management is returning money to the Ministry of Finance, returning money to banks and so on. However, if we are speaking exclusively from this title, from a simplified procedure of accession, an additional 50 million taxpayers' money must be paid here. In fact, taxpayers will pay for it sooner or later. Also, the excuse that Slovenia has been granted greater State aid by the European Commission is not a good excuse. It was then that government, the government of Alenka Bratušek, and then Minister Chufar did not take advantage of all state aid, which does not mean, of course, that they now have the resources and that this state aid is free and that there will be no need to pay for it. Maybe with the delay now, but that doesn't mean everything's okay now. This raises an actual question of doubt and also raises questions economically, not only economically and financially, but also morally and ethically. In fact, let us recall that the State, with this taxpayers, has actually recapitalised two private banks that have been in such a bad situation that they should be bankrupt. At that time, in 2003, it was considered necessary to send a smaller bank, a private bank to bankruptcy and that this would not result in any systemic problems in the area of finance or in a significantly lower confidence in the Slovenian financial or banking system. It was then that the government and, of course, Members and Members – without Members and Members of the Slovenian Democratic Party – decided that the two banks should be recapitalised with approximately EUR 467, Pardon 472 million, and about half a billion of the taxpayer's money went to a private bank. This does not mean, of course, that there were no other solutions, and this does not mean that other solutions would not be better, especially for taxpayers. Today, when it was the best solution then, it is difficult to speak because we do not know what would have happened if another solution had been used if banks had gone bankrupt. We don't know these scenarios, and because of that, saying that this was the best solution, it's too much for my notions and, I'll say, very unilaterally, and if you want, even unprofessionally. The purpose of the controlled liquidation of both banks was to pay off all ordinary creditors. The question here is whether they have actually been paid. As can be seen from this, all creditors are certainly not paid, and from this point of view, the question is whether this has actually been achieved. In fact, three years ago, in 2013, it was said that these two banks would go into liquidation and that nothing would come of it. And as has already been said here, the question, of course, is whether it is the right decision that these two banks have moved to join the Bank's Claims Management Company. Even the professional public, if you like, is here on different shores. On the one hand, we know the position of Banka Slovenije, which was then exceeded or not taken into account – in short, that these claims of both banks are transferred to a new company established by private capital. This was the position of Banka Slovenije for a long time, I think until July 2015. This position was also discussed by the Governor of Banka Slovenije to transfer these claims to a private company, but this idea was actually rejected by the Ministry of Finance. We did not get any arguments as to why and why not and why it was rejected; we got arguments as to why this would be good. Today, we see that this would be good because it would probably not result in the loss of 50 million, which is covered by taxpayers today. However, it is likely that the State would not then pursue that private company which could have been d.o.o. to which these claims would have been transferred, in order to effectively recapitalise this private company. I mean, that would be impossible. But then someone decided, with unknown arguments or at least some detailed analysis of how this could happen, and here came the grey fox and doubt whether this connection between Probanka and Factor Bank to the state-owned company DUTB is really optimal. In other words, if I make two points – in short, we are talking about not having the optimal solutions, because the government as a assembly cannot prove that it is an optimal solution, and secondly, we have non-transparent solutions because the Government does not take into account the conclusions of the National Assembly or the Commission for the Control of Public Finance, which is the Commission of the National Assembly and is concerned about public finances. Since the government doesn't take into account and simply question the National Assembly, we're going our own way. In doing so, the coalition supports the Government and does not, in fact, support the National Assembly, does not support the decisions of the National Assembly in this regard. I think this is actually a very questionable action that is taking place in Slovenia. For this reason, we have also proposed all these conclusions in the Slovenian Democratic Party, we have also proposed other conclusions, some of which I have read to you, which have been adopted by other committees or commissions. Because we are most optimal in these matters, we are taking solutions that will be least painful for taxpayers or taxpayers' money and that the country on the other hand will have money for investments, not just for the rehabilitation of banks. In this regard, we will support a proposal that has already been implemented to a certain extent. We regret that no other proposals have been adopted. I can say that it is with resentment that we accept the fact that the Government simply does not accept the decisions of the National Assembly, in this case the Commission for the supervision of public finances, and does not carry out the procedures in the direction set out by these decisions. Thank you very much.

The government confirmed the option of joining, as it has been shown that such an approach in terms of the overall capital burden for the country and the public financial impact is most appropriate. The advantages of the connection are also that the linkage eliminates the risk that the liquidity aid to banks in the Republic of Slovenia will not be reimbursed and the links will also allow longer time to recoup the assets of the acquired companies and thus greater withdrawal and lower value for the additional capital burden for the owner or taxpayers. In its assessment of the connection made by the Bank of Slovenia as the Supervisory Board of Probanka and Factor Bank, the Bank of Slovenia also assessed that the connection was legally and economically justified and that it followed all legal provisions under the ZGD. At the above-mentioned meetings held in the National Assembly, the Governor also explained the negative effects both banks would have had on the liquidity of the banking system and, given the circumstances in 2013, it would likely also result in uncontrolled departures of investors from the entire banking system and, as a result, in some other banks' inefficiencies. From this point of view, personally to me, this decision on controlled liquidation was correct and in September 2013 this decision was taken that the two banks were going to a supervised liquidation, which was called an exceptional administration, and at the meetings we also received explanations or conclusions from the Governor that you had been successful in that all creditors were paid, additional recapitalisations approved by the European Commission at an additional amount of EUR 76 million had not been made, due to the early repayment of the liabilities would be lower, the DUTB had taken over the bank's unsold assets and, as the manager of this asset, had more time to cash in the bank's assets and for a more favourable upturn. As the Governor has also said, all the procedures envisaged for bank termination have been carried out prematurely and procedures have been put in place to safeguard the interests of banks. According to Banka Slovenije's assessment, the final cost of ending both banks for the budget of the Republic of Slovenia will be equal to the amount of the recapitalisation carried out in December 2013, that is EUR 435 million, the remaining 76 million of the approved recapitalisation. Compared to the cost of bankruptcy – this data was also presented at the Committee on Finance meeting – would be higher by 300 million euros. This assessment takes into account only the costs related to the perspective of both banks, but does not affect the overall banking system. As has already been said, the recapitalisation for the Factor Bank was carried out in the amount of 269 million, Probanks 176 million, the European Commission approved 285 for the Factor Bank and 236 for Probanka. As I said, this state aid has not been fully exploited, but now the key issue here is that after the evaluation is completed, once the definitively known effects of the accession can be seen as a side effect, this has also been shown to us, the connection made is a need to provide additional capital for the DUTB, which should not go beyond the remaining amount of State aid granted. In its February 2016 report, the DUTB Management Board pointed out this; it noted that it would be able to call on the owner to replace part of the transferred capital deficit from both banks of the Republic of Slovenia. Why did this happen? We have also heard today and also on the committee; it is a matter of different valuations of the portfolio transferred from both banks to DUTB. Various methodologies are used by banks, different methodologies are used by DUTB and there is a possible additional need for additional capital for DUTB, but this potential recapitalisation of DUTB due to the connection deficit will be discussed when the DUTB completes the evaluation of the transferred Probanka and Factor Bank items and when the auditor also gives an opinion, namely the auditor who revises the DUTB financial statements. ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u66	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. The debate was substantively rich. I am sorry that there have been many open questions and unanswered questions from previous discussions that have taken place in the working bodies of the National Assembly, so I will make every effort to give as many answers as possible and to give as many precise answers as possible to these open questions. Nevertheless, at the beginning of this work, I would like to stress once again the conclusion of the assessment I gave in the introduction. This is the conclusion, in fact, of the Government's view that in the process of controlled liquidation of the two banks there was a final payment by the depositaries of the two banks, which enabled them to cease. On the other hand, the negative impact of the termination of these two banks on the financial system of the Republic of Slovenia and the financial stability ensured was prevented as a key condition that Slovenia was gradually able to recover. This is a general view of the government generally on the resolution of the banking system, and on the other hand, as regards this particular situation in these two banks and the measures taken by that government. However, if we want to systemise the issues raised during the presentation of the views of Member groups as the views of individual Members and Members in the debate, I think we should segment these issues into three parts: one is the systemic aspects of what the approaches have been used in the decisions taken by these banks; the other is the implementation, the implementation, the implementation measures, how the measures taken have been implemented; and the third, the consequences, the positive and the negative, and the risks involved. As regards the systemic aspects of the measures, we initially said that these were the decisions of the Bank of Slovenia and the decisions of the previous government, which decided to liquidate the two banks under control. Certainly, the situation in the financial markets and even in Slovenia was very uncertain in 2013, practically at the height of the Cyprus financial crisis, when Slovenia was also expected to have impossible access to financial markets. At that moment, the decision-makers of the decision-making period, both the Bank of Slovenia and the Government, decided on a controlled liquidation. Now, whether this was the best possible solution or not, especially given that it was two private banks, that they would probably not be different. I would not like to assess this here, because the Government has not made a statement on this either. What I would say in my personal name is that some alternative solutions could also be used. The liquidation is always difficult or problematic, because banks cease to finance activities, so they stop financing current transactions, stop financing credits, projects that are being built up, stop, the value of this asset is greatly reduced and the economic value of the loans or bank exposures that are given is also greatly reduced. If solutions were to be sought to ensure that these banks continue, in some other form, either by connecting to larger banks, by inviting new owners to make these banks available to investors, which then appear in Slovenia, it would probably be possible to drive things differently, perhaps not by so erosion of the value of these two banks' assets, but by formal means, these decisions were taken and started to be implemented. I think that it was certainly an important element of these decisions, too, that the Government and Banka Slovenije were counting on them to be able to obtain part of the European money from the European Central Bank’s programmes, an instrument of extreme emergency lending. This, while at the time, partially deplored the public financial and current situation, entailed certain obligations, in particular with regard to obligations towards the European Central Bank and the approval of State aid. As a result, only banks that were solvent could be granted an extreme loan under the rules of that time. It is said that the issue of capital deficit was not an open issue and therefore this instrument of extreme urgency had to be combined immediately with the instrument of recapitalisation. Throughout the whole process of implementing these measures, liquidity aid aspects on the one hand through the first intervention of the European Central Bank, followed by the cashing of the State guarantee and the direct exposure of the Ministry of Finance, were in fact weighted for the cashed state guarantee in terms of the divestment of these assets to the two banks and their capital adequacy. The capital here needs to be viewed in this light, as usually seen in banks, as a buffer for the risks arising from the operations of banks. Since this business was limited to asset-purchase, to the sale of assets, it is clear that the liquidation scenario and liquidation values had to be foreseen and, therefore, in the procedures for granting State aid to the European Commission, the European Commission then gave its consent on the basis of a restructuring programme; first for the resolution and then for the supervised termination of the two banks with two measures, liquidity aid and recapitalisation. Liquidity aid was immediately granted; recapitalisation was also carried out in steps; it was assessed according to the methodologies used at the time by the European Commission. The Ministry of Finance in its previous and current composition was relatively conservative, as regards the gradual release of the amount of approved recapitalisations that covered the risks arising from this liquidation scenario of the sale of assets. All of this must have been viewed very much at that time, as the Governor also presented at the home work body of the National Assembly, through the contaminating aspect of other banks, the threat of financial stability. In particular, we stressed in the discussions that the SID Bank was highly exposed as a state bank, which implemented part of its programmes by not lending directly to companies, but by lending through banks and also through these two banks, which were some kind of financial intermediary and also mitigated the risk of SID to the Bank. So much for systemic aspects. Decisions were taken, and when it came to take place this government was, in fact, in the face of a dilemma as to how these measures had already been implemented or completed. It received a proposal from the Bank of Slovenia and there were also various discussions on how this controlled liquidation would have been most appropriate and, in these implementing aspects, it was assessed that the linkage to the bad bank was appropriate, even in this light, because we weighed on one side the risk arising from the ability to pay in full the liquidity loan provided to these two banks and on the other hand the risks associated with the possible capital mismatch of banks or the undercutting of the sale of assets. Thus, from the point of view of the Ministry of Finance, even the risk arising from the potential inability not to pay off credits was greater than the question of recapitalisation, since, on the date of the accession, it was pointed out, for 368 million loans and in the event of the bankruptcy of these two banks, these loans would immediately be shown in national accounts statistics as the deficit of the country's A balance sheet sector. While maintaining the ability to recover liquidity aid and to service, properly restructure and recover liquidity, it in fact maintains the situation that directly such costs or that the potential inability to repay a liquidity loan is not recognised in one step as potential or as a real deficit in the A balance sheet of the country account. While the recapitalisation in this light was a measure granted under State aid by the European Commission, which also requires tax money, the recapitalisation was used at a total of EUR 445 million for both banks and the remaining 76 million. The implementation itself was a number of questions whether Banka Slovenije carried out this most effectively or not, certainly it is always possible to do better than it was done, there are not some valuable judgments, so we did not put one of those that now proved to be the focus of the activities of these compulsory administrations, the liquidation administrations was the rate of cost reduction and the rate of reduction and the optimization of the number of employees. In these segments, any extension of the continuation of the independence of these two institutions would generate additional costs in these two institutions and would further reduce the available buy-in or the loss of the buy-in from the sale of these assets. Thus, more could be done in this part and, above all, too little has been done by one of these two banks to implement the employee optimisation programme, so the number of employees at the merger has increased disproportionately to the DUTB, since the DUTB is the universal legal successor of these two banks and is through only a status change, through commitment and all claims taken over by all employees. This also partly shows the limited manoeuvre that one of these two banks had had since, if it had implemented a programme to reduce the number of employees, it would have spent its resources extra for this and would have been already in the phase, I would have said that before the capital deficit was added, it was greater than it had actually been shown. Thus, efforts have been made in the two segments of the implementation itself from 2013 until the end of 2015 to ensure that the assets are sold as favourably as possible and, on the other hand, clearly, the cost component must also be monitored. The applicant's key observation is that the decision on the simplified connection was taken irresponsibly to cover up the individual aspects of this connection in order to hide the state of the two banks when they joined the DUTB. This audit is defined in Articles 582 and 583 of the Associations when the more complex models of these status changes and connections are, when the shareholders of the merged companies are eliminated either by cash payment or by shares of the acquiring company. In this case, the so-called swaps between the shares of the company taken over with the shares of the acquiring company occur. This requires an audit of the evaluation whether these are objective. In our case, there has been no spending on capital. The minutes show that the most favourable offer is the one sent by Resni, Audit and Consulting, d.o.o. Therefore, the Commission decided to propose to the National Assembly to select this audit company for the audit of the Court’s financial statements. Thank you very much.

Since the resources for the work of the Court of Auditors are determined by the Court of Auditors on a proposal from the Court of Auditors and form an integral part of the State budget, the Court shall, after a review of the National Assembly, submit a report on the chosen audit of the company. For now these funds are not spent on purpose, but in the health treasury they are destroyed for anything. Therefore, in NSa we propose that the health insurance company from these funds paid by entrepreneurs cover the costs in case of injuries due to minor negligence. If this was a matter of greater negligence, this damage would be reimbursed directly by the employer, up to 20 times the minimum pension base, amounting to around 15,000 euros over the thumb. However, when it comes to intentional or intentional injury, the employer will reimburse all treatment costs, even though this should lead to the closure of the company or the cessation of the activity. Today, after practical arrangements are in place, these applications may be disproportionately high without any restrictions. Under the current legislation, in Slovenia, it is not possible to protect the employer's liability in the event of injury at work and occupational disease, as is possible in the case of car insurance in the event of accidents. In Slovenia, we cannot secure regressive liability in any insurance company, for example, by craftsmen and entrepreneurs in neighbouring Austria and Germany. In particular, this means that it is precisely because of this opportunity that they have that they can get a better business environment. Independent entrepreneurs are among the most vulnerable groups of employers who respond with their personal property even in the event of minor negligence. In Novi Slovenije, we have therefore decided to listen to those employers, small craftsmen and entrepreneurs who, due to such claims for reimbursement of costs treated by the Institute of Health Insurance, are at risk of a decline, namely, small craftsmen, expatriates, who are also required by the Department of Health Insurance for a further EUR 70,000, and more, and the country should also be aware of the potential job losses. If such an undertaking closes its business, jobs are lost and all future payments are made to the health department and the damage is twice as much. According to Slovenian legislation, the Slovenian Institute of Health Insurance has the right to claim compensation in the amount of damage if the injury, illness or death of the worker is caused because the appropriate measures for protection and the primary working environment have not been implemented. Of course, there is no problem with that. However, there are certain limits everywhere, and the limits must also be set so that this responsibility can be protected. By amending the legislation we are proposing, we do not want to give any silence and prejudice to the required rules on safety and health at work, but merely to distribute financial responsibility for safety and health at work in an appropriate manner. Each participant must assume his or her share of the responsibility. The purpose of the amendments we are proposing is not to reduce the importance of safety or health at work, nor to reduce the employer's liability standards, but to divide the burden more justly among all involved. Once again, our proposal for a law has made it clear that the cost of treatment from an entrepreneur can be reimbursed. If there is a minor negligence, they shall be reimbursed from the money already collected for salaries, 0.53% for each salary. Secondly, if this is a matter of greater negligence, it is the burden of €15 000 that we can charge to the entrepreneur, but if this is a matter of slander, it is a full cost. This seems to be the only meaningful and just arrangement we have.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u77	Thank you very much. I give the floor to the representative of the Government of Ms Nina Pirnat, State Secretary of the Ministry of Health, to introduce the position on the proposal of the parent working body.
ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u78	Thank you very much. Dear Members and Members! The Government fully opposes the proposal of the Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on Health Care and Health Insurance, which amends the fundamental principle of compensation law, so that the damage caused by it can be compensated. In our opinion, the bill also opposes the basic principles of the Law on Safety and Health at Work and also interferes with the fundamental obligations of the Labour Relations Act. A brief summary of the opinion: The current regulation is based on the fundamental principle of compensation law, so that the damage caused at the level of the damage is reimbursed by the person who caused it. However, the proposal does not follow the principle mentioned above, since it proposes that the Office of Health Insurance of the Republic of Slovenia should have the right to request from the employer the damage caused only when the disease, injury or death of the insured person was caused by the employer’s intention or serious negligence to suspend the implementation of safety or health measures at work. Furthermore, the proposal further limits the employer's responsibility by proposing to limit the amount of damage caused in the event of gross negligence to the 20th timer of the lowest pension base. Indemnity is a civil liability and therefore its purpose is not to punish the responsible person, but to establish the situation prior to the occurrence of the damage. The primary function of compensation law, therefore, is to eliminate the unfair situation in which the injured person was found, thus eliminating his deprivation — damage. This is a so-called compensatory repatriation function of compensation law, and the draft law only partially follows the principles of compensation liability. The fundamental principles of employers' responsibility in ensuring safety and health at work are set out in Article 5 of Council Directive of 12 June 1989 on the introduction of measures to promote improvements in the safety and health of workers at work. In accordance with that provision of the Directive, the employer must in all respects ensure the safety and health of workers in relation to work. The exclusion of the employer's responsibility to ensure safe and healthy work is only possible in accordance with the Directive because of unusual and unforeseen circumstances not controlled by the employer or because of exceptional events. The suspension of prescribed measures to ensure safety and health at work is not one of the circumstances that would allow such exclusion of the responsibility of employers. The applicant further proposes that the light negligence of the employer would be covered by compulsory health insurance, where a specific contribution for injuries at work and occupational diseases is paid to employers. With regard to that point, Article 89 of the Health and Health Insurance Act, which provides that in the cases referred to in Articles 86 and 87, it is considered that the CFSP has suffered injury irrespective of the fact that the health insurance risks are covered. The contribution paid by employers to the Institute of Health Insurance in case of injury at work and occupational illness of the worker is due to the fact that in carrying out the activity, i.e. work, there is a greater likelihood of injury than in those who do not work. In addition to the impact on the economy and the social sector, the proposal also has financial effects, as the funds collected by the CFSP from the collection of contributions to compulsory health insurance are separate funds from the State budget, but are still public funds intended to provide health care to the population. Disposal of these funds at the expense of the lower liability of employers would have an impact on health care and public finances, and thus would be less money for health care. In view of all of this, we reiterate that we do not support the proposal for a law, since the proposal therefore opposes the rules of employers' liability for compensation, the rules of protection and health at work and the obligations of employers arising from the labour relationship.

ParlaMint-SI_2016-06-21-SDZ7-Redna-20.ana.u80	Dear colleagues and respectable colleagues! So why has Nova Slovenija prepared a novel of the Law on Health Care and Health Insurance? On the other hand, with nothing, we stress that we are not making workers' safety worse or reducing employers' responsibilities. Regret claims or claims for damages arising from an accident at work or occupational disease may be lodged against persons responsible for the damage caused under Slovenian law both by the Institute for Health Insurance of Slovenia and by the Institute for Pension and Disability Insurance. The obliging code of the Republic of Slovenia defines compensation liability as an obligation of an individual to remedy the damage to which it is responsible and which arises only if four conditions are met. First, unacceptable behavior, secondly, damage, causation and responsibility. Indemnity liability for health insurance is more precisely regulated by the Health and Health Insurance Act. The CFSP has the right to request from the employer compensation for damages, i.e. amounts paid if the injury, illness or death for the worker is caused by the failure of the employer to take appropriate measures for the safe working environment and for the safety of persons. The proposal of New Slovenia therefore regulates employers' claims for compensation in such a way that they are responsible for injury at work and occupational disease only in the event of gross negligence, while in other cases the costs are reimbursed by the Slovenian Institute of Health Insurance, where employers pay their contributions. The purpose of this type of safe and, in particular, healthy work is to prevent injuries at work and occupational diseases and to ensure social security for those who are injured at work or who are sick at work for a occupational disease, at the same time in a predictable legal and business environment that does not endanger the existence of the cause. Christian Democrats feel that the current legislation is unfair, especially to a small employer who answers unlimitedly. Indemnity contributions can therefore jeopardise the business of some employers, particularly economically weaker, small and medium-sized enterprises. In 2013, the ZZZS recorded an inflow of EUR 1 million 213,000, already EUR 1 million 456 000 in 2014, and EUR 1 million 167 000 in 2015. However, the key question is why insurance companies do not want to enter this market as well. Why this is not interesting to them — because this limitlessness is essentially unlimited and insurance companies do not want to take that high risk. In New Slovenia, we propose that the employer's liability is only in case of occupational illness, injury at work or death due to gross negligence of the employer or intentionally, while in the event of low negligence, the regression requirement is not enforced, as all employers pay a contribution of 0.53% in case of injury at work and occupational illness in the case of compulsory health insurance. These revenues are essentially used to finance all health programmes, not just what is one of them, which is one of the major shortcomings. Employers' liability for compensation under the proposal for the amendment of the law is limited to 20 times if the damage is caused by great negligence and unlimited if the damage is intentionally caused. Therefore, it is precisely the point of the restriction that in individual cases the existence of a cause of injury, especially of small and medium-sized employers, is not jeopardised by the fact that it does not allow them to operate in a stable and predictable manner. We believe that such a proposal, proposed by Nova Slovenije, represents a fairer burden sharing among all stakeholders, and therefore the law deserves support.
